```text
80	\label{sec:crossover}
81	
82	The phase-strategy crossover is model-dependent. For DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at all four sparsity levels: at $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$), with reversal magnitude ranging from $2.2{\times}$ to $4.0{\times}$ across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. Qwen does \emph{not} exhibit this crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$). This divergence suggests the crossover arises from model-specific attention head specialization patterns rather than a universal compound-sparsity property. Full crossover analysis appears in Appendix~\ref{app:crossover-details}.
83	
84	\subsection{Replication Across Training Paradigms}
85	\label{sec:cross-model}
86	
87	DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (distillation-trained) and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (instruction-tuned) share the Qwen2 backbone but differ in training objective, providing a within-architecture-family comparison across training paradigms.
88	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\text{eff}}{=}475$) reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.07$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 10^{-14}$), paralleling DeepSeek at approximately one-quarter to one-sixth the magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing median $\Delta$ trajectories (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z \geq 6.2$, $p < 10^{-9}$; see \S\ref{par:stats}). Random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, becoming significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$--$0.91$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$) but $2.7$--$20{\times}$ weaker than importance-based. A caveat: 45.2\% of Qwen problems yield null mid-phase segments due to low temporal autocorrelation ($r{=}0.718$ vs.\ $0.894$); this does not affect $\Delta$ (Appendix~\ref{app:qwen-null-rate}). Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}.
89	
90	\subsection{Preliminary Cross-Architecture Analysis}
91	\label{sec:cross-arch}
92	
93	Preliminary cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$)\footnote{The $n{=}49$ sample is an initial profiling subset. Full-scale ($n{=}500$) LlamaForCausalLM replication is planned as future work; the current analysis prioritizes within-architecture replication (Qwen2) over cross-architecture breadth.} provides preliminary support for the phase-gated severity pattern under importance-based eviction (post-hoc power $> 0.99$ for within-importance gradient) but is inconclusive for strategy comparisons (power $0.26$--$0.63$); the interaction magnitude is ${\sim}40{\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek, suggesting that quantitative characteristics may be Qwen2-family-specific and that broader architectural diversity (e.g., Mistral, Gemma) is needed for generalization claims. Full results, including per-sparsity breakdowns and a power analysis motivating the sample size needed for definitive cross-architecture claims, appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-arch}.
94	
95	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation: GSM8K}
96	\label{sec:cross-benchmark}
97	
98	To assess task generalization, we evaluate both models on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. On DeepSeek (clean data, no output truncation), importance-based eviction replicates the MATH-500 phase gradient across all four sparsity levels with a strict dose-response: $\Delta{=}0.24$ at $s{=}0.15$ rising to $4.68$ at $s{=}0.30$ (all $p \leq 0.0003$, Wilcoxon). Strategy specificity on GSM8K manifests as a \emph{qualitative} gap: importance-based eviction produces significant phase gradients while random eviction is non-significant across all conditions (CIs spanning zero). We attribute this to \emph{task complexity}: GSM8K's simpler arithmetic is associated with more uniform attention distributions, yielding a flatter sparsity landscape; within MATH-500, longer traces exhibit higher strategy specificity (Spearman $\rho > 0$, $p < 0.02$), supporting cognitive load as the modulating variable. Qwen GSM8K results are inconclusive due to generation truncation (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$2047): while directional consistency holds at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}1.14$--$4.62$; $p < 10^{-4}$), absolute values and phase assignments are unreliable because all outputs hit the token limit. Full tables in Appendix~\ref{app:gsm8k}.
99	
100	\paragraph{GSM8K accuracy under compound sparsity.}
101	A power analysis on GSM8K ($n{=}50$) reveals a strategy-dependent asymmetry in downstream accuracy. Under random head removal, all 8 conditions (2 models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels) are non-significant (max $|d|{=}0.164$). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $n{=}50$ can detect effects of $d \geq 0.40$ ($\alpha{=}0.05$, power${=}0.80$); all random conditions showed $|d| \leq 0.164$, meaning the null results may reflect insufficient power rather than true absence of effect. Under importance-based pruning, Qwen exhibits a medium-sized accuracy degradation at $s \geq 0.25$ ($d \approx 0.63$, power ${>}\,0.99$), consistent with the PPL-based severity gradient observed on MATH-500. DeepSeek importance conditions exhibit a qualitative downward trend in accuracy but are excluded from parametric inference due to heavy-tailed distributions that violate test assumptions.
102	
103	\subsection{Statistical Validation}
104	\label{sec:stat-validation}
105	
106	Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports the paired median difference $\Delta$ under importance-based eviction for both models at $n{=}496$/$475$. All eight conditions yield CIs excluding zero; within-importance dose-response $d_z$ (s015 vs.\ s030) is $0.94$ (DeepSeek) and $0.92$ (Qwen), both large effects. Importance-based eviction produces significantly larger phase gradients than random at all four sparsity levels ($p_{\text{Bonf}} \ll 10^{-10}$, Wilcoxon; permutation tests in Appendix~\ref{app:permutation-details}).
107	
108	\begin{table}[t]
109	\centering
110	\small
111	\caption{Median of paired differences ($\Delta_i = \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction with bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}
112	\label{tab:cohens-d}
113	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
114	\toprule
115	$s$ & DS ($n{=}496$): $\widetilde{\Delta}$\; [95\% CI] & QW ($n{=}475$): $\widetilde{\Delta}$\; [95\% CI] \\
116	\midrule
117	0.15 & $0.39$\; $[0.35,\; 0.44]$ & $0.07$\; $[0.05,\; 0.09]$ \\

119	0.25 & $3.99$\; $[3.53,\; 4.36]$ & $1.07$\; $[0.98,\; 1.18]$ \\

123	\end{tabular}
124	\end{table}
125	
126	\subsection{Answer-Region Analysis}
127	\label{sec:answer-region}
128	
129	Across both models, 93.2\% of answer tokens (\verb|\boxed{}| markers) fall within the high-sparsity phase, where compound interaction is minimal ($G \approx 1$). This non-uniformity identifies \emph{where} severity concentrates: reasoning-trace tokens show $5.7$--$43.5{\times}$ PPL degradation vs.\ $1.3$--$5.3{\times}$ for answer tokens (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). However, reasoning-trace degradation may produce \emph{silent reasoning errors}---correct formatting with corrupted intermediate logic \cite{lanham-2023-faithfulness, turpin-2023-biased-cot}---a failure mode that PPL detects but answer-level accuracy may miss.
130	
131	\subsection{Downstream Accuracy Validation}
132	\label{sec:accuracy-validation}
133	
134	To validate that the PPL-based severity gradient translates to downstream task performance, we evaluate MATH-500 accuracy under compound sparsity (importance-based eviction, \texttt{max\_new\_tokens}${=}$8192; Table~\ref{tab:accuracy}). Qwen's minimal truncation ($\leq 4\%$) provides a clean PPL-accuracy bridge.
135	Qwen ($n{=}100$; truncation $\leq 4\%$) shows gradual decline from $46\%$ (dense) through $s{=}0.25$ ($\delta{=}-4$ to $-13$\,pp) with a sharp cliff at $s{=}0.30$ ($19\%$, $\delta{=}-27$\,pp). DeepSeek ($n{=}50$) degrades more steeply ($74\% \to 4\%$) with a dual-regime structure: at $s \leq 0.20$, degradation is primarily truncation-driven (non-truncated accuracy remains $68$--$71\%$ at $s{=}0.15$--$0.20$ vs.\ $88\%$ dense); at $s \geq 0.25$, both truncation and reasoning quality collapse simultaneously (Appendix~\ref{app:accuracy-details}).
136	The two models exhibit distinct failure modes reflecting training-paradigm dependence: DeepSeek failures are \emph{truncation-dominated} (truncation $16\% \to 76\%$), while Qwen failures are \emph{wrong-answer-dominated} (truncation $\leq 4\%$, reasoning quality degrades directly). Given minimal truncation, Qwen accuracy data provide a reliable PPL-accuracy bridge; DeepSeek accuracy results, confounded by truncation rates of $16\%$--$76\%$, should be interpreted as qualitative observations only.
137	
138	We provide a correlational mechanistic analysis in terms of importance discrimination collapse in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
139	
```

```text
1	\section{Importance Discrimination Collapse under Compound Sparsity}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	The mechanisms discussed here are \emph{correlational diagnostics} that illuminate but do not fully explain the $15$--$17{\times}$ severity divergence between strategies. Both strategies exhibit near-identical Gini drops ($<10\%$ difference), yet severity diverges by $15$--$17{\times}$---an order-of-magnitude mismatch indicating that aggregate concentration collapse alone is insufficient. The critical differentiator is \emph{structural specificity} of importance-based pruning, which systematically removes high-attention-score heads (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}).
5	
6	\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}
7	\label{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}
8	
9	Head pruning systematically reduces the discriminative power of cumulative importance scores, suggesting a mechanism for the phase-strategy interaction. The Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek, $6$--$17\%$ relative to dense baseline) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen, $2$--$4\%$) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas in all 16 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}). Random eviction shows near-identical Gini drops (e.g., DeepSeek $s{=}0.25$: $-0.065$ vs.\ $-0.060$), confirming that collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}. Yet importance-based severity is $15$--$17{\times}$ larger---the amplification arises because importance-based eviction \emph{depends on} the very scores that pruning degrades.
10	
11	This pattern---\emph{importance discrimination collapse}---reflects eviction degenerating into noise-sensitive selection as score distributions approach uniformity. The effect is phase-dependent: low-sparsity phases have broadly distributed attention where collapse has maximum impact; high-sparsity phases preserve concentration. \cite{hies-2025} report this collapse under single-axis pruning; we extend it to the compound setting.
12	
13	To provide quantitative evidence for structural specificity, we analyzed the attention Gini coefficients of importance-pruned versus retained heads. Importance pruning (Frobenius norm criterion) systematically removes heads with lower Gini coefficients---i.e., heads with more dispersed attention patterns (Mann-Whitney $U$, $p < 10^{-28}$; rank-biserial $r = 0.62$--$0.76$ across all 8 conditions). Random pruning, by contrast, samples heads with Gini indistinguishable from the population mean (200-trial verification). The Frobenius norm and Gini share sensitivity to attention magnitude; the large effect size ($r > 0.6$) nonetheless indicates strong structural separation.
14	
15	We note that this evidence is correlational; causal validation through controlled manipulation of importance-score entropy is an important direction for future work.
16	%% TODO: Add per-phase Gini results when available from Agent computation.
17	
18	\subsection{Entropy Redistribution: A Model-Dependent Secondary Effect}
19	\label{sec:entropy-redistribution-main}
20	
21	As a supplementary observation, head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads on DeepSeek ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Appendix~\ref{app:analysis}, Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}). However, entropy redistribution shows model-dependent behavior---the direction reverses on Qwen at $s{=}0.25$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = +0.045$)---replicating in only 2/4 Qwen conditions and thus only partially consistent. Importance discrimination collapse remains the more robust, model-independent correlate, though it partially accounts for rather than fully explains the severity divergence.
22	
```

```text
No GPU/NVIDIA/CUDA mentions in any .tex files ✓

Let me note the key findings:

1. **analysis.tex line 16**: There's a TODO comment: `%% TODO: Add per-phase Gini results when available from Agent computation.` — This is a residual TODO that should be addressed when c5_per_phase_gini completes. Not a correction error, but worth noting.

2. **Paper is internally consistent**: All headline numbers match across abstract, intro, and results tables.

3. **LLaMA n=49 references**: Still in abstract, intro, results, limitations. 

This is expected since c8_llama_n250 is still running. I'm also noticing a discrepancy between paper_framing.md and the actual paper tables — the framing document lists DS median differences as 0.32→1.15→3.97→9.94, but both the main results table and the Cohen's d validation table show 0.39→1.21→3.99→8.48, which appear to be the correct values based on the statistical validation caption. — this explicitly defines it as median of paired differences, matching the paper_framing definition. So the computation should be the same, but the numbers differ.

Wait, actually looking more carefully at the tables, the main results caption uses $\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$, while the Cohen's d table uses $\widetilde{\Delta}$ with $\Delta_i = \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{low}} - \mathrm{II}^{(i)}_{\textit{high}}$ — so one's the difference of medians and the other's the median of paired differences. But both tables show identical Δ values (0.39, 1.21, 3.99, 8.48), which shouldn't happen unless they're computing the same thing.

Let me check the actual numbers: for s=0.15 importance, the table shows Δ = 0.39 [0.35, 0.44], but II_high = 0.003 and II_low = 0.357, which gives 0.354 as the difference of medians — not 0.39. So the Δ column isn't actually showing the difference of the medians displayed in that row, which means the II_high and II_low columns must be showing something else, like marginal medians rather than the values used in the Δ calculation. So Table 1's notation is misleading even though the actual computation is correct — it's the paired median difference, not the median of the differences, which are different things. Table 2 makes this explicit with clearer notation. But there's a real discrepancy in the actual values between paper_framing and the paper itself: at s015 I'm seeing 0.39 versus 0.32, and at s030 it's 8.48 versus 9.94, which are substantially different. Paper_framing claims to reference registry cohens_d data and was updated recently, so this inconsistency is puzzling, but I'm not going to dig deeper into whether it's pulling from different profiling runs or something else.

Now I'm going through the review checklist to verify all the corrections were actually implemented. R10-C1 removed MUSTAFAR from the bibliography and all tex files. R10-W1 eliminated any overclaiming language like "novel" or "first." R10-W3 added the H2O confound limitation. R10-W4 scrubbed all GPU and NVIDIA references. R10-W5 included Bonferroni corrections, bootstrap confidence intervals, and the Jonckheere-Terpstra test throughout. R10-W6 improved clarity by explicitly framing this as a diagnostic study.

But I'm finding a few issues that need attention. There's a lingering TODO comment in analysis.tex about adding per-phase Gini results that should be removed before submission. The paper_framing.md document has different DS delta values than what's actually in the paper, so those need to be reconciled. And the table caption uses a specific notation for delta that I should verify is consistent with how it's defined elsewhere.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Confirmed: both scripts do the same thing (generate s020/s025 completions for 50 MATH-500 problems), both on GPU 1.

- Script 1 (`c10_gen_s020_s025.py`): Uses `CONDITION_SPECS` from `run_accuracy_eval`, runs both conditions for each problem (like the original c10 script). At problem 5/50 for the first condition (s020). Outputs to `results_c10_lang_s020_s025.json`.


- Script 2 (`gen_c10_s020_s025.py`): Processes conditions sequentially instead, currently at problem 4/50 on the first condition, writing to separate output files for each condition.

Both are using identical settings—same model, dataset, seed, and token limits—which makes this a clear duplicate. I need to flag this as a warning and decide which one to keep.

The main distinction is that Script 1 interleaves the two sparsity levels per problem like the original approach, while Script 2 runs them one after another. Script 1 is further along at problem 5, and Script 2 has a more consolidated summary format across all sparsity levels.

Both are running full text generation with language detection on the first 100 characters, which is better than the earlier CPU-based approach. Script 2's methodology applies importance collection per problem to build masks, then generates with deterministic sampling and language detection on the initial text.

The real issue is GPU memory — both processes together are consuming around 32 GB on a 97 GB card, which creates contention and slows everything down even though it's not hitting OOM. I should recommend terminating one of them since Script 2 has the cleaner analysis pipeline but Script 1 is further into the work.

I also need to check whether c5_per_phase_gini is still running.

Looking at the nvidia-smi output, GPU 0 is handling the two c5 processes while GPU 1 has the c10 jobs and GPUs 2-3 are running c8_llama_n250_fixed — all within the allocated cuda:0-3 range, so no constraint violations there.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0112yVyTqnpWGhqiSVdp4PAL`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Long-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.
5	Two complementary responses---\emph{attention head pruning} \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \emph{KV cache eviction} \cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---are increasingly deployed simultaneously as \emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.
6	
7	Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.
8	This assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose chain-of-thought generations \cite{wei-neurips2022-cot} alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.
9	Phase-aware methods like ThinKV \cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from score collapse \cite{hies-2025}, greedy bias \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, and temporal dynamics \cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple axes are composed.
10	If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
11	
12	We find that compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of the three-way interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
13	Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity reasoning phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.
14	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), the paired median difference rises from $0.32$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
15	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; all $p < 0.005$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$), where the phase gradient direction is consistent but strategy${\times}$phase interaction is weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models.
16	Random eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude than importance-based (where both strategies reach significance at $s \geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is strategy-specific.
17	Cross-benchmark validation on GSM8K reveals consistent phase gradient direction for both models (DeepSeek: $\Delta$ $0.241 \to 4.682$; Qwen: $0.039 \to 2.092$), while strategy specificity (importance/random gradient ratio) attenuates from $15$--$36{\times}$ on MATH-500 to $2.3$--$3.8{\times}$ on GSM8K, indicating that phase modulation generalizes but its magnitude is task-dependent.
18	
19	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this phase--strategy divergence. Both strategies exhibit consistent Gini drops across all tested conditions, yet severity diverges by $2.7$--$15{\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.
20	
21	Our contributions:
22	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	    \item We characterize a \textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\rho{=}1.0$; $\Delta$: $0.32 \to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
24	    \item We replicate the pattern \textbf{across models and architectures}: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$) and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B ($n{=}49$; consistent direction, weaker strategy${\times}$phase contrast; \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
25	    \item We identify \textbf{importance discrimination collapse} via per-phase Gini analysis as a correlational diagnostic: consistent collapse across models and strategies, with the structural specificity of importance-based pruning corresponding to the severity divergence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
26	    \item We validate the phase gradient on \textbf{GSM8K} for both models, finding that phase modulation is benchmark-robust while strategy specificity is task-dependent (\S\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28

      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/introduction.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \\cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.\nTwo complementary responses---\\emph{attention head pruning} \\cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \\emph{KV cache eviction} \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---are increasingly deployed simultaneously as \\emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}. Recent evaluations confirm that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately under compound compression \\cite{unicomp-2026}, and that per-dimension safe zones intersect to form a smaller joint safe region \\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026}.\n\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}, despite evidence that pruning sensitivity depends on representational phase \\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} and that no single sparse attention strategy dominates across generation stages \\cite{sparse-frontier-2025}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose chain-of-thought generations \\cite{wei-neurips2022-cot} alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}.\nPhase-aware methods like ThinKV \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} apply different KV strategies per phase but without considering compound interaction, while importance-based eviction itself faces challenges from score collapse \\cite{hies-2025}, greedy bias \\cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026}, and temporal dynamics \\cite{lethe-aaai2026}. These methods each optimize a single pruning axis; we characterize the interaction when multiple axes are composed.\nIf compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWe find that compound sparsity severity is an emergent property of the three-way interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity reasoning phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.\nOn DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), the paired median difference rises from $0.32$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.048 \\to 2.619$; all $p < 0.005$), with preliminary cross-architecture evidence from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, $n{=}49$), where the phase gradient direction is consistent but strategy${\\times}$phase interaction is weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models.\nRandom eviction develops a detectable phase gradient only above $s \\approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\\times}$ smaller magnitude than importance-based (where both strategies reach significance at $s \\geq 0.25$), confirming that the dominant gradient is strategy-specific.\nCross-benchmark validation on GSM8K reveals consistent phase gradient direction for both models (DeepSeek: $\\Delta$ $0.241 \\to 4.682$; Qwen: $0.039 \\to 2.092$), while strategy specificity (importance/random gradient ratio) attenuates from $15$--$36{\\times}$ on MATH-500 to $2.3$--$3.8{\\times}$ on GSM8K, indicating that phase modulation generalizes but its magnitude is task-dependent.\n\nPer-phase Gini analysis identifies \\emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this phase--strategy divergence. Both strategies exhibit consistent Gini drops across all tested conditions, yet severity diverges by $2.7$--$15{\\times}$, implicating the structural specificity of importance-based pruning as the key differentiator.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We characterize a \\textbf{phase-modulated compound severity gradient}: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonic dose-response ($\\rho{=}1.0$; $\\Delta$: $0.32 \\to 9.94$) with a phase gradient $2.7$--$15{\\times}$ larger than random eviction where both strategies are significant ($s \\geq 0.25$) on MATH-500; the gradient direction is consistent on GSM8K, while strategy specificity attenuates (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).\n    \\item We replicate the pattern \\textbf{across models and architectures}: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\\Delta$: $0.048 \\to 2.619$; $\\rho{=}1.0$) and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B ($n{=}49$; consistent direction, weaker strategy${\\times}$phase contrast; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n    \\item We identify \\textbf{importance discrimination collapse} via per-phase Gini analysis as a correlational diagnostic: consistent collapse across models and strategies, with the structural specificity of importance-based pruning corresponding to the severity divergence (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n    \\item We validate the phase gradient on \\textbf{GSM8K} for both models, finding that phase modulation is benchmark-robust while strategy specificity is task-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-benchmark}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 28,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 28
    }
  },

1	\begin{abstract}
2	Compound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.
3	We find that compound sparsity severity is not a fixed constant but an emergent property of the interaction among eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level.
4	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B (MATH-500), importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonically increasing phase--severity gradient: the paired median difference $\Delta$ rises from $0.32$ at sparsity $s{=}0.15$ to $9.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; all Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$).
5	Random eviction develops a detectable gradient only above $s \approx 0.20$, at $2.7$--$15{\times}$ smaller magnitude.
6	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($\Delta$: $0.048 \to 2.619$; $\rho{=}1.0$), shows consistent phase gradient direction on GSM8K for both models, and exhibits compatible direction on LlamaForCausalLM ($n{=}49$; strategy${\times}$phase interaction weaker than on Qwen2-architecture models).
7	Per-phase Gini analysis identifies \emph{importance discrimination collapse}---the degeneration of head importance scores toward uniformity under compound stress---as a correlational diagnostic of this severity divergence.
8	These findings reveal that compound degradation concentrates in specific reasoning phases, challenging the assumption of uniform compound interaction.
9	\end{abstract}
10

1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity}
6	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction develops a detectable but weak phase gradient above $s \approx 0.20$: at $s \leq 0.20$, $\Delta$ is non-significant ($0.01$--$0.03$; CIs span zero); at $s \geq 0.25$, $\Delta$ becomes significant ($0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$) but $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than under importance-based eviction. Both strategies exhibit monotonic dose-response (Jonckheere-Terpstra $z{=}6.2$--$33.7$, all $p < 10^{-9}$), but importance masking effect sizes are an order of magnitude larger.
9

11	\centering
12	\small
13	\caption{Median of per-problem paired severity differences ($\Delta$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$, MATH-500).}

15	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}

17	Strategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\

23	\end{table}
24	
25	
26	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification}
27	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
28	
29	Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the per-phase breakdown. Under importance-based eviction, the median II in the low-sparsity phase rises from $0.357$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $8.456$ ($s{=}0.30$), while the high-sparsity phase remains near zero ($0.003$--$0.038$), yielding paired median differences $\Delta$ from $0.39$ to $8.48$ (all $p \ll 10^{-10}$). Random eviction shows a detectable but much weaker phase gradient at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.26$--$0.51$; $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.006$), $15$--$17{\times}$ smaller than importance-based at matched sparsity. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes the dose-response relationship for both models. The per-problem amplification ratio $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) provides a complementary ratio perspective and is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:g-metric}.
30	Under the multiplicative null ($r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$; Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null}), the phase-severity gradient is preserved ($G_{\times}{=}21$--$64{\times}$); we report additive-null values throughout as the more conservative estimates.

Under importance-based eviction, $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $8.48$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $22{\\times}$ amplification (all Bonferroni-corrected $p \\ll 10^{-10}$). - DS s030 importance Δ = 8.48 [7.75, 9.69]

Looking at the registry data for the early experiments, I need to figure out which numbers are actually correct. The paper_framing references 0.32/1.15/3.97/9.94, but the tables show 0.39/1.21/3.99/8.48 — these are different metrics or computations. The 0.39 value in the table represents the median of per-problem paired differences, which is distinct from whatever the framing numbers represent.

The discrepancy between 0.32 and 0.39 could stem from the sample size difference (n=496 in the table versus possibly n=50 in the pilot data), and with heavy-tailed distributions, even small sample changes can shift the median noticeably.

Now I need to verify what the registry actually shows for the main DeepSeek n=500 experiment to confirm whether those median_diff values of 0.32/1.15/3.97/9.94 are correct.

```text
1	\section{Compound Sparsity Formulation}
2	\label{app:formulation-details}
3	
4	\paragraph{Attention head pruning.}
5	Heads are ranked by Frobenius norm importance $w_j^{(\ell)} = \|\mathbf{A}_j^{(\ell)}\|_F$ \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}; a binary mask $m_j^{(\ell)} \in \{0,1\}$ retains the top-$(1{-}s)\cdot H$ heads:
6	\begin{equation}
7	    \tilde{\mathbf{o}}_j^{(\ell)} = m_j^{(\ell)} \cdot \mathbf{o}_j^{(\ell)}.
8	    \label{eq:head-mask}
9	\end{equation}
10	
11	\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}
12	A position mask $\mathbf{m}_k \in \{0,1\}^T$ retains the top-$(1{-}s)\cdot T$ positions (BOS always preserved):
13	\begin{equation}
14	    \tilde{a}_{ij} = a_{ij} \cdot m_{k,j}, \quad \forall\, i, j.
15	    \label{eq:kv-mask}
16	\end{equation}
17	Under importance-based eviction, each position $j$ is scored by its cumulative attention mass with position normalization:
18	\begin{equation}
19	    \mathrm{imp}(j) = \frac{\sum_{\ell, h, i} a_{ij}^{(\ell,h)}}{T - j},
20	    \label{eq:kv-importance}
21	\end{equation}
22	where the denominator compensates for causal bias---earlier positions are attended to by more queries \cite{thinkv-iclr2026, defensivekv-iclr2026}. Under random eviction, positions (excluding BOS) are evicted uniformly at random.
23	
24	\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}
25	Applying both mechanisms simultaneously yields compound sparsity. The two axes interact: head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering the importance ranking for KV eviction. Prior work \cite{spark-aaai2026} reports super-additive joint effects but treats interaction magnitude as a fixed property of $s$.
26	
27	\paragraph{Attention sparsity metric.}
28	For each query position $i$ and head $h$, we compute the fraction of key positions receiving negligible attention:
29	\begin{equation}
30	    \sigma_i^{(h)} = \frac{1}{T} \sum_{j=1}^{T} \mathbf{1}\!\left[a_{ij}^{(h)} < 0.01 \cdot \max_k a_{ik}^{(h)}\right].
31	    \label{eq:attn-sparsity}
32	\end{equation}
33	The per-position sparsity $\sigma_i = \frac{1}{H}\sum_h \sigma_i^{(h)}$ averages across all heads. Tokens are partitioned into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity phases by the tercile boundaries of $\{\sigma_i\}$; segments shorter than 10 tokens are merged into adjacent phases.
34	
35	\section{Multiplicative Null Verification}
36	\label{app:multiplicative-null}
37	
38	We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\mathrm{II}_{\times} = r_b - r_a \cdot r_k$. Notably, $\mathrm{II}_{\times} < 0$ across all phases and sparsity levels: the compound interaction is \emph{sub-multiplicative} everywhere---actual compound degradation is less severe than the multiplicative prediction. $G_{\times}$ therefore quantifies the ratio of sub-multiplicative deviations between phases, not super-multiplicative amplification: a value of $G_{\times}{=}30$ means the low-sparsity phase deviates from the multiplicative null by $30{\times}$ more than the high-sparsity phase. Under importance-based eviction, $G_{\times}{=}21.1$--$36.2{\times}$ (DeepSeek) and $43.9$--$64.0{\times}$ (Qwen) across $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, yielding a combined range of $21$--$64{\times}$. Although compound effects do not exceed the multiplicative prediction in any phase, the low-sparsity phase's deviation from the multiplicative null is $21$--$64{\times}$ larger than the high-sparsity phase's, indicating that the phase-gated severity gradient is preserved under the multiplicative null. These $n{=}500$ exact values are more conservative than the preliminary $n{=}50$ calibrated estimates ($\approx$51--183${\times}$), reflecting reduced variance at full sample size. Values are computed using aggregate per-phase PPL ratios; per-problem exact computation is left for future work. Random eviction remains flat ($G_{\times} \approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text as the more conservative baseline: it assumes zero interaction (compound loss equals the sum of individual losses), so rejection ($\mathrm{II} > 0$) establishes super-additivity regardless of null specification. The qualitative conclusions---phase-modulated severity, strategy asymmetry, and crossover---are preserved under the multiplicative null.
39	
40	% Bootstrap and permutation details moved to main text \S\ref{sec:stat-validation}
41	
```

1	
2	\section{Phase-Strategy Crossover Details}
3	\label{app:crossover-details}
4	
5	On DeepSeek ($n{=}496$), a strategy ranking reversal emerges at $s{=}0.25$ (Figure~\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.021$ vs.\ $1.113$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($3.971$ vs.\ $1.514$---a $2.6{\times}$ reversal; both $p < 10^{-4}$). This crossover is observed at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $4.0{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $3.4{\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $2.6{\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $2.2{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (decreasing as random eviction severity rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse-main}). Qwen does not exhibit a crossover: importance-based eviction produces lower II than random in both phases across all sparsity levels (e.g., at $s{=}0.25$: high $0.004$ vs.\ $1.108$; low $1.053$ vs.\ $1.769$).
6	
7	\begin{figure}[t]
8	\centering
9	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}
10	\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$ on DeepSeek ($n{=}496$). Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $2.6{\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}
11	\label{fig:crossover}
12	\end{figure}

The paired permutation test on this subset ($n{=}49$; Table~\ref{tab:permutation}) provides bootstrap 95\% CIs for the strategy contrast ($\widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{imp}} - \widetilde{\Delta}_{\text{rand}}$), all excluding zero after Bonferroni correction.

44	
45	\section{Cross-Training-Paradigm Replication Details}
46	\label{app:cross-model-details}
47	
48	Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This within-architecture-family comparison enables assessment of training methodology effects on the compound sparsity interaction, though causal attribution is limited by the observational design.
49	
50	Despite lacking explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\S\ref{par:phase-segmentation}). Twenty-five of the 500 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}475$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\ $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek).
51	
52	\paragraph{Per-phase effective sample size.}
53	\label{app:qwen-null-rate}
54	Table~\ref{tab:qwen-effective-n} reports the per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, defined as the number of problems with non-null phase segments (segment length $\geq$ \texttt{min\_phase\_length}). The mid-phase null rate (45.2\%) reflects problems where mid-sparsity tokens do not form contiguous runs above the merging threshold; this does not affect the low--high contrast ($\Delta$) used in the primary analysis.
55

72	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} reports the cross-training-paradigm comparison. Qwen reproduces the monotonic severity gradient at ${\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek's magnitude. Figure~\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes this comparison.

Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-quarter to one-sixth of DeepSeek's.}

95	
96	\paragraph{Per-problem concordance.}
97	The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\rho = -0.107$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking.
98	
99	\paragraph{Random eviction control.}
100	As a control, random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$ ($\Delta$: $-0.01$, $0.02$; CIs span zero) but significant at $s \geq 0.25$ ($\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; $\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$3.5{\times}$ weaker than importance-based ($0.39$ vs.\ $1.07$ at $s{=}0.25$; $0.91$ vs.\ $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$), supporting strategy-specific amplification.
101	
102	
103	\section{Permutation Test Details}
104	\label{app:permutation-details}
105	
106	For each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\Delta_i^{(\text{strat})} = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\widetilde{\Delta} = \text{median}_i\!\bigl(\Delta_i^{(\text{imp})} - \Delta_i^{(\text{rand})}\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.
107	
108	Per-problem distributions of the paired severity difference $\Delta_i = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under importance-based eviction are right-skewed at all sparsity levels, with mean/median ratios ranging from $1.2{\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $1.6{\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$) for DeepSeek and $1.1{\times}$ to $1.4{\times}$ for Qwen.
109	
110	The permutation null distributions are approximately symmetric around zero at all sparsity levels. At $s{=}0.15$, the observed statistic falls at the $99.92$th percentile of the null ($p_{\text{Bonf}} = 0.0032$); at $s \geq 0.20$, no permutation exceeds the observed statistic.
111	
112	Table~\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \to 0.69 \to 1.66 \to 6.70$) supports that compound severity amplification strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot.
113

118	\label{tab:permutation}
119	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}
120	\toprule
121	$s$ & Observed $\widetilde{\Delta}$ & 95\% CI & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
122	\midrule
123	0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\
124	0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
125	0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
126	0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
127	\bottomrule
128	\end{tabular}
129	\end{table}
130	
131	\begin{figure}[t]

133	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_bootstrap.pdf}
134	\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results. Horizontal bars show 95\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\widetilde{\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero, supporting significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}
135	\label{fig:permtest-forest}
136	\end{figure}
137	
138	
139	\section{Cross-Architecture Analysis}
140	\label{app:cross-arch}
141	
142	To test whether the phase-modulated severity pattern extends beyond the Qwen2 architecture, we replicate the experiment on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, 8B parameters; $n{=}49$ paired problems). Table~\ref{tab:llama-cross-arch} reports the strategy-dependent severity comparison across sparsity levels.
143	
144	The evidence for a strategy contrast on LLaMA is mixed: at $s{=}0.15$, the bootstrap CI excludes zero ($[0.002, 0.043]$) but the Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon test is non-significant ($p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.219$); conversely, at $s{=}0.30$ the CI spans zero ($[-0.069, 0.544]$) while the Wilcoxon test reaches significance ($p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$). At intermediate levels ($s \in \{0.20, 0.25\}$), neither CI nor Wilcoxon reaches significance. The median paired difference increases monotonically ($\widetilde{\Delta}$: $0.020 \to 0.356$), consistent with a genuine but weak trend---providing preliminary support for the cross-architecture hypothesis while underscoring the substantially weaker interaction on LlamaForCausalLM.\footnote{The CI/Wilcoxon discrepancy reflects differing power profiles under small $n$ with heavy-tailed distributions: the percentile bootstrap CI is based on the resampled median distribution and is less sensitive to tail observations, while the Wilcoxon signed-rank test uses signed ranks and is more sensitive to tail outliers. This inconsistency itself supports the conservative ``mixed/weak evidence'' interpretation.}
145	
146	The absolute magnitude is architecture-dependent: LLaMA severity differences are ${\sim}40{\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek and ${\sim}7{\times}$ smaller than Qwen. Notably, random eviction on LLaMA also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient ($d_z$: $0.35 \to 0.44$), substantially reducing the strategy contrast that is the hallmark of the phase-strategy interaction on Qwen2 models. This suggests that the strength of the strategy$\times$phase interaction---not merely the severity gradient itself---is architecture-dependent. The directional pattern---importance producing larger phase gradients than random---is qualitatively consistent across all three models, but the LLaMA evidence should be interpreted as preliminary given the weak interaction magnitude and partial statistical significance.
147	
148	\begin{table}[t]
149	\centering
150	\small
151	\caption{Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM). $\widetilde{\Delta}$: median paired difference (importance $-$ random) with bootstrap 95\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$); $p$: Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$ ($k{=}4$). $n{=}49$ paired problems.}
152	\label{tab:llama-cross-arch}
153	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
154	\toprule
155	$s$ & $\widetilde{\Delta}$\; [95\% CI] & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
156	\midrule
157	0.15 & $0.020$\; $[0.002,\; 0.043]$ & $0.219$ \\
158	0.20 & $0.022$\; $[-0.090,\; 0.187]$ & $0.253$ \\
159	0.25 & $0.095$\; $[-0.004,\; 0.303]$ & $0.083$ \\
160	0.30 & $0.356$\; $[-0.069,\; 0.544]$ & $\mathbf{0.029}$ \\
161	\bottomrule
162	\end{tabular}
163	\end{table}
164	
165	\paragraph{Post-hoc power analysis.}
166	We computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\Delta_{\text{imp}} - \Delta_{\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is supported, but the strategy$\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.
167	
168	
169	\section{Analysis: Importance Discrimination Collapse}
170	\label{app:analysis}
171	
172	\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}
173	\label{sec:discrimination-collapse}
174	
175	Head pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, collapsing the score distribution. Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.
176	
177	\begin{table}[t]
178	\centering
179	\caption{Importance discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\Delta\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}
180	\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}
181	\small
182	\begin{tabular}{llrr}
183	\toprule
184	Model & $s$ & $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\Delta\mathrm{CV}$ \\
185	\midrule
186	DeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\
187	DeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\
188	DeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\
189	DeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\
190	\midrule
191	Qwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\
192	Qwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\
193	Qwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\
194	Qwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\
195	\bottomrule
196	\end{tabular}
197	\end{table}
198	
199	When importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced collapse has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, consistent with $G \approx 1$.
200	
201	\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}
202	\label{sec:entropy-redistribution}
203	
204	Beyond importance discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:
205	\begin{equation}
206	    \mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \log a_{ij}^{(h)}
207	    \label{eq:attn-entropy}
208	\end{equation}
209	and compute $\Delta\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.
210	
211	For DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing importance discrimination collapse.
212	
213	For Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). The $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low: $+0.042$, mid: $+0.034$, high: $+0.017$). We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.
214	
215	We characterize importance discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver of the phase-strategy interaction, with entropy redistribution as a secondary amplifier that is consistent in DeepSeek but model-dependent in Qwen.
216

220	\label{tab:entropy-deltas}

230	\midrule
231	Qwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\
232	Qwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\
233	Qwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\

236	\end{tabular}
237	\end{table}

The paired permutation test on this subset ($n{=}49$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}) provides bootstrap 95\\% CIs for the strategy contrast ($\\widetilde{\\Delta}_{\\text{imp}} - \\widetilde{\\Delta}_{\\text{rand}}$), all excluding zero after Bonferroni correction.\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Training-Paradigm Replication Details}\n\\label{app:cross-model-details}\n\nBoth models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This within-architecture-family comparison enables assessment of training methodology effects on the compound sparsity interaction, though causal attribution is limited by the observational design.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{par:phase-segmentation}). Twenty-five of the 500 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}475$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}496$ for DeepSeek).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-phase effective sample size.}\n\\label{app:qwen-null-rate}\nTable~\\ref{tab:qwen-effective-n} reports the per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, defined as the number of problems with non-null phase segments (segment length $\\geq$ \\texttt{min\\_phase\\_length}). The mid-phase null rate (45.2\\%) reflects problems where mid-sparsity tokens do not form contiguous runs above the merging threshold; this does not affect the low--high contrast ($\\Delta$) used in the primary analysis.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-phase effective sample size for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n_{\\text{eff}} = 475$). Phase segmentation is performed once on dense-model attention patterns and is independent of sparsity level.}\n\\label{tab:qwen-effective-n}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nPhase & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nHigh & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\nMid  & 274 & 274 & 274 & 274 \\\\\nLow  & 475 & 475 & 475 & 475 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} reports the cross-training-paradigm comparison. Qwen reproduces the monotonic severity gradient at ${\\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek's magnitude. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes this comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of median compound severity difference ($\\Delta$) under importance-based eviction.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}496$) & 0.39 & 1.21 & 3.99 & 8.48 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($n{=}475$)         & 0.07 & 0.31 & 1.07 & 3.16 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-training-paradigm comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction ($n{=}496$/$475$). Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-quarter to one-sixth of DeepSeek's.}\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Per-problem concordance.}\nThe per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.107$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking.\n\n\\paragraph{Random eviction control.}\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen mirrors DeepSeek: non-significant at $s \\leq 0.20$ ($\\Delta$: $-0.01$, $0.02$; CIs span zero) but significant at $s \\geq 0.25$ ($\\Delta{=}0.39$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$; $\\Delta{=}0.91$, $p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-13}$). Where significant, the random phase gradient is $2.7$--$3.5{\\times}$ weaker than importance-based ($0.39$ vs.\\ $1.07$ at $s{=}0.25$; $0.91$ vs.\\ $3.16$ at $s{=}0.30$), supporting strategy-specific amplification.\n\n\n\\section{Permutation Test Details}\n\\label{app:permutation-details}\n\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nPer-problem distributions of the paired severity difference $\\Delta_i = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under importance-based eviction are right-skewed at all sparsity levels, with mean/median ratios ranging from $1.2{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $1.6{\\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$) for DeepSeek and $1.1{\\times}$ to $1.4{\\times}$ for Qwen.\n\nThe permutation null distributions are approximately symmetric around zero at all sparsity levels. At $s{=}0.15$, the observed statistic falls at the $99.92$th percentile of the null ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} = 0.0032$); at $s \\geq 0.20$, no permutation exceeds the observed statistic.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) supports that compound severity amplification strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ profiling subset, $10{,}000$ permutations, Bonferroni-corrected). The monotonic $\\Delta$ pattern is supported at $n{=}496$ (Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii}).}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero, supporting significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Architecture Analysis}\n\\label{app:cross-arch}\n\nTo test whether the phase-modulated severity pattern extends beyond the Qwen2 architecture, we replicate the experiment on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM, 8B parameters; $n{=}49$ paired problems). Table~\\ref{tab:llama-cross-arch} reports the strategy-dependent severity comparison across sparsity levels.\n\nThe evidence for a strategy contrast on LLaMA is mixed: at $s{=}0.15$, the bootstrap CI excludes zero ($[0.002, 0.043]$) but the Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon test is non-significant ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.219$); conversely, at $s{=}0.30$ the CI spans zero ($[-0.069, 0.544]$) while the Wilcoxon test reaches significance ($p_{\\text{Bonf}}{=}0.029$). At intermediate levels ($s \\in \\{0.20, 0.25\\}$), neither CI nor Wilcoxon reaches significance. The median paired difference increases monotonically ($\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: $0.020 \\to 0.356$), consistent with a genuine but weak trend---providing preliminary support for the cross-architecture hypothesis while underscoring the substantially weaker interaction on LlamaForCausalLM.\\footnote{The CI/Wilcoxon discrepancy reflects differing power profiles under small $n$ with heavy-tailed distributions: the percentile bootstrap CI is based on the resampled median distribution and is less sensitive to tail observations, while the Wilcoxon signed-rank test uses signed ranks and is more sensitive to tail outliers. This inconsistency itself supports the conservative ``mixed/weak evidence'' interpretation.}\n\nThe absolute magnitude is architecture-dependent: LLaMA severity differences are ${\\sim}40{\\times}$ smaller than DeepSeek and ${\\sim}7{\\times}$ smaller than Qwen. Notably, random eviction on LLaMA also exhibits a non-trivial phase gradient ($d_z$: $0.35 \\to 0.44$), substantially reducing the strategy contrast that is the hallmark of the phase-strategy interaction on Qwen2 models. This suggests that the strength of the strategy$\\times$phase interaction---not merely the severity gradient itself---is architecture-dependent. The directional pattern---importance producing larger phase gradients than random---is qualitatively consistent across all three models, but the LLaMA evidence should be interpreted as preliminary given the weak interaction magnitude and partial statistical significance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture validation on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (LlamaForCausalLM). $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$: median paired difference (importance $-$ random) with bootstrap 95\\% CI ($B{=}10{,}000$); $p$: Bonferroni-corrected Wilcoxon signed-rank $p$ ($k{=}4$). $n{=}49$ paired problems.}\n\\label{tab:llama-cross-arch}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$\\; [95\\% CI] & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & $0.020$\\; $[0.002,\\; 0.043]$ & $0.219$ \\\\\n0.20 & $0.022$\\; $[-0.090,\\; 0.187]$ & $0.253$ \\\\\n0.25 & $0.095$\\; $[-0.004,\\; 0.303]$ & $0.083$ \\\\\n0.30 & $0.356$\\; $[-0.069,\\; 0.544]$ & $\\mathbf{0.029}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Post-hoc power analysis.}\nWe computed post-hoc power via bootstrap simulation ($B{=}10{,}000$) to assess what claims the $n{=}49$ sample can and cannot support. For the within-importance phase gradient (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} > 0$), power exceeds $0.99$ at all four sparsity levels, indicating that the sample is sufficient to detect the importance-based severity gradient. For strategy comparisons (testing $\\Delta_{\\text{imp}} - \\Delta_{\\text{rand}} > 0$), power ranges from $0.26$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $0.63$ ($s{=}0.30$), indicating that the sample is underpowered for detecting strategy-level differences at their observed effect sizes. Extrapolating from the observed effect sizes and variance, $n \\geq 200$ paired problems would be needed to achieve $0.80$ power for the strategy comparison at $s{=}0.30$. This motivates the conservative interpretation in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}: the within-importance gradient is supported, but the strategy$\\times$phase interaction requires larger-scale replication.\n\n\n\\section{Analysis: Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{app:analysis}\n\n\\subsection{Importance Discrimination Collapse}\n\\label{sec:discrimination-collapse}\n\nHead pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, collapsing the score distribution. Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, indicating that importance discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Importance discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}\n\\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWhen importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced collapse has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, consistent with $G \\approx 1$.\n\n\\subsection{Entropy Redistribution}\n\\label{sec:entropy-redistribution}\n\nBeyond importance discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \\log a_{ij}^{(h)}\n    \\label{eq:attn-entropy}\n\\end{equation}\nand compute $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.\n\nFor DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.047, -0.040]$; Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), indicating that importance-based pruning systematically selects heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing importance discrimination collapse.\n\nFor Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). The $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low: $+0.042$, mid: $+0.034$, high: $+0.017$). We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.\n\nWe characterize importance discrimination collapse as the robust, model-independent driver of the phase-strategy interaction, with entropy redistribution as a secondary amplifier that is consistent in DeepSeek but model-dependent in Qwen.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Attention entropy deltas ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$) under importance-based eviction. DeepSeek shows consistent entropy compression; Qwen partially replicates with a reversal at $s{=}0.25$.}\n\\label{tab:entropy-deltas}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.040$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.047$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.045$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.044$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.007$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
      "numLines": 238,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 238
    }
  },

1	\section{Benjamini-Hochberg FDR Correction}
2	\label{app:bh-fdr}
3	
4	Table~\ref{tab:bh-fdr} compares Bonferroni ($k{=}4$, per model$\times$strategy family) and Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction across all 44 statistical tests. BH-FDR recovers 5 additional significant results beyond Bonferroni, all in low-effect-size conditions (random eviction or cross-training-paradigm random comparisons). All 32 Bonferroni-significant results remain significant under BH-FDR.
5	
6	\begin{table}[t]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{Bonferroni vs.\ BH-FDR correction summary ($\alpha{=}0.05$, 44 tests). ``BH only'' denotes tests significant under BH-FDR but not Bonferroni.}
10	\label{tab:bh-fdr}
11	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
12	\toprule
13	Category & Bonf.\ sig. & BH-only sig. \\
14	\midrule
15	Per-condition Wilcoxon ($k{=}16$) & 10 & 2 \\
16	Imp vs.\ rand ($k{=}8$) & 8 & 0 \\
17	Adjacent sparsity ($k{=}12$) & 10 & 1 \\
18	Cross-paradigm Mann-Whitney ($k{=}8$) & 4 & 2 \\
19	\midrule
20	Total ($k{=}44$) & 32 & 5 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	The 5 BH-only results are: DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25$ and $s{=}0.30$ per-condition Wilcoxon ($p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.064$/$0.065$, BH $q{=}0.002$), DeepSeek random $s{=}0.25 \to 0.30$ adjacent-sparsity step ($p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.078$, BH $q{=}0.002$), and cross-training-paradigm random $s{=}0.25$/$s{=}0.30$ Mann-Whitney ($p_{\text{Bonf}}{=}0.66$/$0.17$, BH $q{=}0.018$/$0.005$). All involve random eviction---the weakest effects---indicating that Bonferroni is conservative but does not miss any importance-based findings.
26	
27	
28	\section{Minimum Phase Length Sensitivity}
29	\label{app:mpl-sensitivity}
30	
31	Table~\ref{tab:mpl-sensitivity} reports the paired median difference $\Delta$ under three \texttt{min\_phase\_length} (mpl) settings across all 16 conditions (2 models $\times$ 2 strategies $\times$ 4 sparsity levels). Conclusions are robust: all importance-based conditions remain significant at $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 10^{-10}$ across mpl $\in \{5, 10, 20\}$; random conditions maintain the same significance pattern (non-significant at $s \leq 0.20$, significant at $s \geq 0.25$). Median $\Delta$ values vary by $<5\%$ between mpl settings for DeepSeek importance, supporting that the severity gradient is not an artifact of the merging threshold.
